I lose all sense of where I actually am. One second I'm scrubbing a pan or looking at the road, and the next second I am right back in that office, feeling that exact same terror, waiting for the worst to happen. It's just... I can't even fully describe how completely it takes over my entire reality. You would think that after a year and a half I'd be able to tell the difference between a memory and real life, but when it happens, there is no difference. None at all. My brain and my body are absolutely convinced that it's happening all over again right in that very second.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body, watching yourself, when you're reliving the moment when the former employee pulled the gun?\n\nPatient: Outside of my body? No... no, I don't think so. I mean, I've heard people talk about that kind of thing on television, you know, where they say they float up to the ceiling and look down at themselves during a car crash or a surgery or something awful like that. But for me? No. \n\nWhen it happens, when I'm right back in that moment with the shouting and the panic... I am very much inside my own body. That's almost the worst part of it, honestly. I feel every single physical sensation of it. I feel my own heart just hammering against my ribs, so hard it actually hurts my chest. I feel the sweat on my palms, I feel my knees getting weak and shaky... I'm completely trapped right inside myself, feeling every ounce of that terror just coursing through my veins. \n\nSo, no, I'm definitely not watching it from the outside like it's a movie or something happening to someone else. I wish I was. Lord, I really wish I could just detach from it like that. But I am right there, firmly planted in my own skin, living through the absolute worst moments of my life, feeling completely powerless all over again. I'm just... I'm right there in the middle of it, doctor. I never leave my body. It's more like my body just hijacks my mind and drags me right back to that awful day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're in a daze or disconnected from the world around you when you're not reliving the moment when the former employee pulled the gun?\n\nPatient: Disconnected? Like, if I'm at the grocery store, I'm constantly scanning the aisles, looking at everyone who walks by, checking to see where the exits are... No, I'm fully there. I know exactly what day it is, I know exactly where I am, I feel the breeze on my skin, I hear the neighbor's dog barking two houses down. I'm very grounded in reality, for better or worse. Sometimes I think it would be nice to just sort of... float away for a little while, you know? Just completely zone out and not have to be so alert all the time. My husband, he can just sit in his recliner and stare at the wall for twenty minutes, completely lost in thought, not hearing a word I say. So, no, I wouldn't say I feel disconnected or in a daze at all. If anything, I'm a little too connected to everything happening around me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or responsible in any way for what happened with the former employee?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No. No, I really don't. I was just doing my job, executing company policy exactly the way I was trained to do it. He made the choice to bring a weapon into that building. I didn't make him do that. Sometimes my sister will ask me, 'Don't you wish you had just given him another chance?' And I have to tell her, no, because that's not how running a business works. You don't keep a disruptive employee just because you're afraid they might do something unthinkable. You just... you just don't ever expect the unthinkable to actually happen. \n\nI know people sometimes blame themselves in these situations, you know, they agonize over what they could have said differently or done differently, but I just don't have that. I've run that afternoon through my head a million times, obviously, but I never once thought, 'I caused this.' Because I didn't. The only person responsible for what happened that day is him. I just happened to be the one sitting across the desk when the final notice was handed down. If it wasn't me, it would have been the district manager, and I'd hate to think of him having to go through this instead, but it certainly wasn't my fault.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid thinking about or talking about what happened with the former employee?\n\nPatient: Avoid thinking or talking about it? Oh, absolutely. I mean, that's practically the whole reason I'm sitting here talking to you right now. And it's not just the buildings. If someone on the news starts talking about workplace security, or if there's a segment on active shooter drills... I hate doing it, but I just can't put myself in that environment. \n\nAnd as for talking about it... well, if someone brings it up, if they say, 'Hey, aren't you the woman from that company where the incident happened?' or if they start asking questions about that afternoon... My sister actually pulled me aside a few weeks ago because we were at a family gathering and I was just sitting in the corner by myself. She wanted to know why I wasn't in the kitchen directing traffic and organizing the food like I normally do. I used to be the one leading everything—family events, volunteer drives, board meetings—I was always the one taking charge and getting things done. And now? Now people are noticing that I just pull back. I just retreat instead of leading like I used to. It makes me wonder why I even bother trying to keep up with it, honestly, when I'm just standing there feeling nothing at all. You remember how good it used to feel, and then you're just staring at a pot of boiling water, feeling absolutely hollow.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel ashamed of what happened with the former employee?\n\nPatient: Ashamed? I don't even know what to call it. It's this suffocating feeling, like all the air has been sucked out of the room, and my heart starts racing, and my chest gets tight. It's like I'm right back in that moment, and I just can't bear to feel that way. \n\nAnd it's the exact same thing with the people. If someone even starts asking about the day it happened, I don't think they're going to hurt me, but I know that if they finish their sentence, if they make me talk about it or even think about it, I'm going to spiral into that awful, suffocating place again. So, I make some excuse and get out. I'll say anything—my phone, my car, a headache—just to escape having to feel those feelings. It’s exhausting, honestly. Just the sheer amount of mental energy it takes to constantly scan my environment, to anticipate what someone might say, to plan my escape routes... it's draining every ounce of energy I have left. I just don't want to feel that way anymore, so I just avoid it all.\n\nDoctor: Can you tell me what happened on the day that you're referring to, the day that it seems like everything changed for you?\n\nPatient: Well, doctor, it was... it was a Tuesday. I remember that because Tuesdays are usually when we do the big vendor reviews, and I had been prepping for it all morning. The weather was actually really nice, which is funny how you remember those little things, isn't it? Anyway, I was in the main office area, just wrapping up some paperwork at my desk. There was a former employee... he had been let go a few weeks prior. I won't get into all the details of his termination, but let's just say it was a long time coming and he wasn't happy about it. \n\nHe came into the building, which he shouldn't have been able to do, but that's a whole other issue with the security protocols we had in place at the time. He was shouting, looking for the HR director, but she wasn't there. So he saw me. And he came right up to my desk. He was so angry, just red in the face, screaming about how we ruined his life. I stood up, trying to use my \"manager voice,\" you know, trying to de-escalate the situation like they teach you in those seminars. I told him he needed to leave, that we could talk outside. \n\nAnd then... he reached into his jacket. I saw the metal. I saw the gun. \n\nAnd doctor, this is the part that is just so incredibly frustrating for me, because I've always prided myself on having a steel-trap memory. I mean, I can recall inventory numbers from five years ago. I can tell you every single mistake the company blamed me for afterward—how they said I didn't handle the security breach right, how I should have locked down the floor faster, all of that nonsense. But the actual confrontation? Right after I saw that gun... it's just gone. \n\nIt's like someone took an eraser to my brain. I know there was shouting. I know people were running. But my own experience in those minutes... my mind just goes completely blank. I've laid awake for hours, night after night, staring at the ceiling, just trying so hard to piece it together. Did I freeze? Did I try to talk to him again? Did someone else step in? I have absolutely no idea. No matter how hard I try to force myself to remember, it's just a black hole. The next thing I actually, truly remember is sitting on the curb outside, shivering even though it was warm out, with a paramedic wrapping a blanket around my shoulders. But that gap... that missing chunk of time right when he pulled the weapon... it drives me absolutely crazy that I can't remember it.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself reliving the moment when the former employee pulled the gun, feeling like you're back in that situation again?\n\nPatient: Yes. Yes, I do. And it’s... it’s honestly one of the hardest parts to deal with, because it just happens completely out of nowhere. I don't even have to be thinking about work or... or him, or anything remotely related to the company. I could just be standing at my kitchen sink, doing something completely mindless. I've always been particular about keeping a tidy house, so I’ll just be standing there, washing dishes, maybe scrubbing a coffee mug and looking out the window at the neighborhood, minding my own business. \n\nOr I'll be out running errands, driving past a warehouse gate—not even the one I used to work at, just any standard industrial gate with the chain-link and the little guard shack—and suddenly, it's like a switch flips in my brain. \n\nThe scene just pops back into my head, and it is so... it's so sharp, doctor. It's not just a passing thought or a regular memory. It’s like I am instantly transported. I can actually hear the panic in people’s voices all around me, that specific pitch of terror when everyone realized what he was pulling out of his jacket, right before my memory cuts out. And my body reacts before I can even stop it. My chest will suddenly get so tight, like someone is pulling a heavy, thick strap around my ribs, and I feel exactly like I’m right back there in that moment. I'll have to put the dish down, or if I'm in the car, I have to grip the steering wheel as hard as I can just to remind myself where I actually am, because the feeling of being right back in that office is just so overwhelming. It takes me completely by surprise every single time.\n\nDoctor: Do you have nightmares about what happened with the former employee?\n\nPatient: Well, sleep... sleep is just a whole ordeal now, doctor. I mean, I used to be the kind of person who could just hit the mattress and be out cold for eight solid hours. When you're managing operations and dealing with vendors all day, you really need your rest, and I never used to have a problem getting it. But now? Now I go through my whole evening routine, I make sure the doors are locked, I turn off all the screens, I get into bed... and nothing. \n\nMost nights I just lie there, staring up at the ceiling, feeling so incredibly tired in my bones, but completely wired at the same time. It's like my brain just refuses to shut off, just humming with this awful nervous energy no matter how exhausted my body is. I've tried reading, I've tried tea, but I just lie there.\n\nAnd then, on the off chance I actually do manage to drift off, it's never a peaceful sleep. I wouldn't say it's always an exact, perfect replay of him standing at my desk, but... well, I wake up from these awful dreams where I can hear the company security radios crackling. You know that sharp, static sound they make when someone keys the mic in a panic? I hear that loud and clear in my sleep, and I hear people shouting. Just all this chaotic shouting echoing everywhere around me. \n\nI'll jolt awake from that, just completely on edge, and I'll look at the clock, and it's always way before daylight—maybe three or four in the morning, pitch black outside. And once I'm up from one of those dreams, that's it for me. The night is over. I can't go back to sleep to save my life. I just lie there in the dark waiting for the sun to come up, and when I finally drag myself out of bed to start the day, I feel like I never really rested at all. It's just so frustrating to start every single morning already feeling completely depleted because I can't even get any peace in my own bed.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been avoiding places and people that remind you of the incident at your former workplace?\n\nPatient: Well, if you really want to know the exact timeline... it started right after the company let me go. Which, by the way, I still can't fully wrap my head around, even now. I mean, I gave them years of my life, managing their operations, keeping everything running smoothly, and after I was the one who had to stand there and face that man with a gun, they turned around and fired *me*. They actually blamed me for the whole thing and terminated my contract. \n\nThat was... gosh, it's been about a year and seven months ago now. This whole avoidance thing has been going on since I was fired after that armed incident, a year and seven months ago. \n\nAnd ever since that happened, since they handed me my papers and pushed me out the door, I just... I can't do it. I can't go near that industrial park. I used to take the interstate exit that goes right past the main warehouse, but now I will drive twenty or thirty minutes out of my way, taking all these winding residential backroads, just to make sure I don't even see the street sign for that avenue. It adds so much time to my errands, but I literally cannot force myself to drive that way. \n\nAnd the people? Forget it. There was a woman, Linda from accounting, we used to get coffee every Thursday morning before the vendor meetings. She tried calling me a few times after they let me go, leaving voicemails, saying she just wanted to check in. But I just deleted them. I ignored her calls, I ignored the emails from the old suppliers... I just can't talk to her. I can't talk to any of them. If I even see someone who looks like one of our old delivery drivers when I'm out at the grocery store, my stomach just drops and I will abandon my shopping cart right there in the produce section and walk out to my car. So, yes, it's been a year and seven months of just completely rearranging my entire daily routine to avoid absolutely anyone and anything that even remotely brings me back to that place.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever find yourself reliving the moment when the former employee pulled the gun, feeling like you're back in that situation again, in a way that feels like it's happening all over again right now?\n\nPatient: Well, yes, exactly, doctor. That's... well, that's what I was just trying to explain a little bit ago. It is exactly like that. It’s not just remembering a bad day, it’s actually living it again. \n\nLike I said, I’ll just be standing at the sink, washing the dishes, completely out of nowhere, or maybe driving past a warehouse gate that has that same chain-link fencing, and it just hits me. And it is so incredibly sharp. I mean, I don't just remember the panic in people's voices, I actually *hear* it. I hear them shouting and panicking like they are standing right next to me in my kitchen, or sitting right there in the passenger seat of my car. It's not an echo from the past, it's happening right then and there. \n\nAnd my chest, it just tightens up so fast and so hard, exactly like I'm back there in that room with him. I lose all sense of where I actually am. One second I'm scrubbing a pan or looking at the road, and the next second I am right back in that office, feeling that exact same terror, waiting for the worst to happen. It's just... I can't even fully describe how completely it takes over my entire reality. You would think that after a year and a half I'd be able to tell the difference between a memory and real life, but when it happens, there is no difference. None at all. My brain and my body are absolutely convinced that it's happening all over again right in that very second.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're outside of your body, watching yourself, when you're reliving the moment when the former employee pulled the gun?\n\nPatient: Outside of my body? No... no, I don't think so. I mean, I've heard people talk about that kind of thing on television, you know, where they say they float up to the ceiling and look down at themselves during a car crash or a surgery or something awful like that. But for me? No. \n\nWhen it happens, when I'm right back in that moment with the shouting and the panic... I am very much inside my own body. That's almost the worst part of it, honestly. I feel every single physical sensation of it. I feel my own heart just hammering against my ribs, so hard it actually hurts my chest. I feel the sweat on my palms, I feel my knees getting weak and shaky... I'm completely trapped right inside myself, feeling every ounce of that terror just coursing through my veins. \n\nSo, no, I'm definitely not watching it from the outside like it's a movie or something happening to someone else. I wish I was. Lord, I really wish I could just detach from it like that. But I am right there, firmly planted in my own skin, living through the absolute worst moments of my life, feeling completely powerless all over again. I'm just... I'm right there in the middle of it, doctor. I never leave my body. It's more like my body just hijacks my mind and drags me right back to that awful day.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever feel like you're in a daze or disconnected from the world around you when you're not reliving the moment when the former employee pulled the gun?\n\nPatient: Disconnected? In a daze? Well... no, not really. I mean, I wouldn't say I walk around in a fog or anything like that. Actually, it's almost the opposite most of the time. \n\nI'm usually hyper-aware of everything going on around me. Like, if I'm at the grocery store, I'm constantly scanning the aisles, looking at everyone who walks by, checking to see where the exits are... I'm very dialed in to my surroundings, probably too much. I don't really have those moments where I just blank out or feel like the world isn't real, unless I'm having one of those flashbacks I just told you about. \n\nBut just on a normal Tuesday afternoon, if I'm folding laundry or sitting on the porch? No, I'm fully there. I know exactly what day it is, I know exactly where I am, I feel the breeze on my skin, I hear the neighbor's dog barking two houses down. I'm very grounded in reality, for better or worse. Sometimes I think it would be nice to just sort of... float away for a little while, you know? Just completely zone out and not have to be so alert all the time. My husband, he can just sit in his recliner and stare at the wall for twenty minutes, completely lost in thought, not hearing a word I say. I used to get annoyed by it, but lately I find myself almost jealous. I just don't have that luxury anymore. My brain is always on, always processing, always making sure I'm safe. So, no, I wouldn't say I feel disconnected or in a daze at all. If anything, I'm a little too connected to everything happening around me.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel guilty or responsible in any way for what happened with the former employee?\n\nPatient: Guilty? No. No, I really don't. I mean, I've thought about that, of course, because you try to make sense of something so completely senseless, but... no. \n\nI was the operations manager, and part of my job—a part I never liked, but a part I had to do—was handling terminations when things just weren't working out. And we had done everything by the book with him. I mean, absolutely everything. There were verbal warnings, written write-ups, human resources was copied on every single email... we gave him every opportunity to correct his behavior on the floor. So when the time came that we had to finally let him go, it wasn't a surprise, or at least it shouldn't have been to him. I was just doing my job, executing company policy exactly the way I was trained to do it. You can't manage a large team and just let things slide, or the whole operation falls apart. \n\nSo, no, I don't feel responsible for what he did. I really don't. He made the choice to come back. He made the choice to bring a weapon into that building. I didn't make him do that. Sometimes my sister will ask me, 'Don't you wish you had just given him another chance?' And I have to tell her, no, because that's not how running a business works. You don't keep a disruptive employee just because you're afraid they might do something unthinkable. You just... you just don't ever expect the unthinkable to actually happen. \n\nI know people sometimes blame themselves in these situations, you know, they agonize over what they could have said differently or done differently, but I just don't have that. I've run that afternoon through my head a million times, obviously, but I never once thought, 'I caused this.' Because I didn't. The only person responsible for what happened that day is him. I just happened to be the one sitting across the desk when the final notice was handed down. If it wasn't me, it would have been the district manager, and I'd hate to think of him having to go through this instead, but it certainly wasn't my fault.\n\nDoctor: Do you avoid thinking about or talking about what happened with the former employee?\n\nPatient: Avoid thinking or talking about it? Oh, absolutely. I mean, that's practically the whole reason I'm sitting here talking to you right now. I spend half my life just trying to dodge anything that even remotely reminds me of that day, or that job, or... well, just about everything connected to it, really. \n\nIt's exhausting, honestly. I used to be such a go-getter, always in the thick of things, but now? Now, if I even have to drive past an office park that looks similar to ours—you know, the ones with those big glass fronts and the little manicured bushes by the entrance—I will literally take a detour that adds twenty minutes to my trip just so I don't have to look at it. And it's not just the buildings. If someone on the news starts talking about workplace security, or if there's a segment on active shooter drills... I have to scramble for the remote and change the channel immediately. I can't even listen to the words without feeling my chest tighten up. \n\nAnd networking events? Forget about it. I used to go to those local business luncheons all the time, shaking hands, talking about supply chains and quarterly goals. Now, if one of my old colleagues invites me to grab a coffee anywhere near a work site, I just make up some excuse. I tell them I have a doctor's appointment, or I'm waiting on a plumber, or my sister needs me to help her with something... I just lie. I hate doing it, but I just can't put myself in that environment. \n\nAnd as for talking about it... well, if someone brings it up, if they say, 'Hey, aren't you the woman from that company where the incident happened?' or if they start asking questions about that afternoon... I just shut down. I will literally turn around and walk away, or I'll suddenly remember I left something in my car, or I'll say I need to use the restroom and then I just leave. I just get out of there as fast as I physically can. I don't want to recount the story, I don't want to see the pity in their eyes, and I definitely don't want to invite those memories back into my head. I spend so much energy trying to keep that door closed, doctor. I just... I do everything in my power to keep it shut tight.\n\nDoctor: Have you noticed any significant problems in your relationships or daily activities because of your reactions to reminders of the incident?\n\nPatient: Oh, absolutely. It’s... it’s really taking a toll on just about everything, to be completely honest with you. It's actually incredibly frustrating for me, because I’ve always been such a capable person. I mean, I’ve been in operations management for over twenty years, I know how to run things, how to organize people. But lately? I’ve actually had to turn down job interviews. Just last month, a recruiter reached out about a really fantastic regional manager role, and it would have been perfect for me, but I just... I had to tell them I wasn't available. Because the truth is, I know I can't reliably get through a standard business meeting or a site visit right now without just completely shutting down or having to physically leave the room if something reminds me of that day. How can you manage a facility if you have to bolt for the door during a routine walk-through? You just can't. \n\nAnd it’s not just paid work, either. I’ve completely stopped taking on my volunteer responsibilities. I used to be so active in the community, you know? I was part of these mentoring circles for young women entering the business world, which I absolutely loved doing. I was always the one organizing the workshops and booking the venues. But I’ve had to step away from all of that. Even with the groups I haven't officially quit yet, and even with my own family, I’m just... I’m so much less present. I might be physically sitting in the room, but I'm not really there. \n\nPeople have definitely started noticing, too. My sister actually pulled me aside a few weeks ago because we were at a family gathering and I was just sitting in the corner by myself. She wanted to know why I wasn't in the kitchen directing traffic and organizing the food like I normally do. I used to be the one leading everything—family events, volunteer drives, board meetings—I was always the one taking charge and getting things done. And now? Now people are noticing that I just pull back. I just retreat instead of leading like I used to. It’s like I’ve lost that whole side of my personality because I’m so worried about putting myself in a situation where I might get overwhelmed and have to run away. So, yes, it’s definitely causing major problems in my life. I just don't know how to be the person I was before.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: Well, it's a little complicated, because it’s not exactly that I’ve just stopped doing things or lost interest in the sense that I refuse to do them. I actually still try to do the things I used to love. But... it’s like the actual feeling is just completely gone. \n\nTake my music, for example. I used to put on salsa music all the time. I'd have it playing loud in the house, and it would just instantly lift me up, you know? Give me all this energy and make me feel alive. And my painting, too. I’ve done watercolors for years, just little landscapes and things, and I used to find so much peace and satisfaction sitting at my easel on a weekend. And cooking Sunday dinner... oh, that was my absolute favorite part of the week. I’d spend the entire day prepping, getting a big roast going, making sure the house smelled wonderful for when the family came over. I loved it. It brought me so much genuine happiness to do those things.\n\nBut now? Now I just... I go through the motions. I really do try to keep it up. I’ll put the salsa music on, I’ll get my brushes out, I’ll buy all the expensive ingredients for a big Sunday meal and stand there chopping vegetables for an hour... but that good feeling just doesn’t come the way it used to. At all. I hear the music, but it’s just noise. I look at the colors on the palette, and they just look dull. I serve this beautiful dinner to my family, and I just feel completely empty inside. \n\nIt’s incredibly frustrating because I know I’m doing all the exact same steps I used to do, I'm executing it perfectly, but the joy is just entirely missing. I keep waiting for that spark, that moment where I feel like my old self and actually enjoy the afternoon, but it just stays flat. It makes me wonder why I even bother trying to keep up with it, honestly, when I'm just standing there feeling nothing at all. You remember how good it used to feel, and then you're just staring at a pot of boiling water, feeling absolutely hollow.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel ashamed of what happened with the former employee?\n\nPatient: Ashamed? No... no, I really wouldn't use that word. I mean, I’ve spent countless hours turning the whole thing over and over in my head, as you can probably imagine, but shame isn't what I feel. You see, I’ve been in operations management for a long time. I started right out of high school, worked my way up from the absolute bottom, so I know how to handle personnel. I’ve dealt with difficult employees, layoffs, heated disciplinary actions... you name it, I’ve managed it. So when I look at what happened with him, I know, logically, that I followed all the standard procedures. I didn't do anything wrong to bring that on. \n\nShame implies that I made some terrible mistake or that I'm embarrassed by my own actions, and I'm really not. I mean, my sister actually asked me something similar a while back, if I felt guilty or ashamed, and I told her exactly what I'm telling you. I was literally just doing my job. I think I was reviewing the quarterly supply logs that afternoon, just like any other normal day. You can't feel ashamed for just sitting at your desk trying to keep the facility running. \n\nIt’s more just... a profound frustration, I guess? Because as a manager, your entire purpose is to fix problems, to anticipate issues, and to keep things orderly, and this was just completely, wildly out of my control. It goes against everything I'm trained to do. But no, the shame belongs entirely to him and the choices he made. I don't carry any of that for him. I just... I just have to deal with the mess it left behind, you know? But ashamed of myself? No, absolutely not.\n\nDoctor: Are there activities that you used to enjoy, but now find you have no interest in doing at all?\n\nPatient: No, no, that's... I guess that's really what I was trying to explain just a minute ago. It’s not that I have absolutely zero interest, or that there's something I look at and say, \"I am never doing that again because I just don't care.\" I do care. I really do. \n\nI mean, I'm a manager, right? My whole life, my entire career since I was eighteen years old, has been about pushing through and getting things done, so I don't just abandon things completely. I don't have a list of hobbies that I've just thrown in the trash. Like, I still buy the paperback mystery novels I used to read out on the porch, and I still maintain my flowerbeds out front. I'm out there weeding them every single Saturday morning just like I have for the last fifteen years. I haven't just thrown my hands up and walked away from anything entirely, because that's simply not who I am. \n\nI still have the... the drive to *want* to do them, if that makes sense? I want to be interested. I want to feel that engagement. So I wouldn't say I have no interest at all in anything. It's just that the payoff isn't there anymore. The feeling is missing, exactly like I was saying with the Sunday dinners and the painting. But the actual interest, the motivation to at least attempt it? No, I haven't lost that. I still try every single day. I really do. I just won't let myself give up on those things, even if they feel empty right now."
